Explanation: A precedent guides a judgment; a prototype guides or informs a design.

Quick lesson: Both serve as an earlier model or reference for a later decision or creation.

Word Analogy — Question 95 C

Correct answer: C. Summer

Explanation: Hibernation is commonly associated with winter; aestivation is commonly associated with hot or dry summer conditions.

Quick lesson: These are periods of dormancy associated with different seasons.

Word Analogy — Question 96 A

Correct answer: A. Self-importance

Explanation: An altruist is characterized by selflessness; an egotist is characterized by self-importance.

Quick lesson: The relationship is a person and the trait that defines that person.

Word Analogy — Question 97 B

Correct answer: B. Conflict

Explanation: A euphemism softens harshness in language; diplomacy helps soften or manage conflict between parties.

Quick lesson: Both involve tactfully reducing the impact of something unpleasant or confrontational.

Word Analogy — Question 98 A

Correct answer: A. Foreigners

Explanation: Hydrophobia is fear of water; xenophobia is fear or dislike of foreigners or strangers.

Quick lesson: The Greek roots identify the object of the fear: water and foreign people.

Word Analogy — Question 99 B

Correct answer: B. Novelty

Explanation: Orthodox ideas follow tradition; innovative ideas introduce novelty or new approaches.

Quick lesson: The relationship contrasts adherence to established ways with the introduction of something new.

Word Analogy — Question 100 B

Correct answer: B. Introduction

Explanation: An epilogue provides a conclusion; a prologue provides an introduction.

Quick lesson: These are opposite structural parts of a written work: one closes it and the other introduces it.

Symbolic Logic — Question 1 B

Correct answer: B. p → q

Explanation: The phrase “if p, then q” is written as p → q. Here, raining is the condition and a wet road is the result.

Quick lesson: In a conditional statement, the condition after “if” comes first, followed by an arrow to the result.

Symbolic Logic — Question 2 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∨ ¬q

Explanation: By De Morgan’s law, the negation of p ∧ q is ¬p ∨ ¬q.

Quick lesson: To negate an AND statement, change AND to OR and negate each part.

Symbolic Logic — Question 3 B

Correct answer: B. False

Explanation: A conjunction (AND) is true only when both parts are true. Since q is false, p ∧ q is false.

Quick lesson: For AND, both statements must be true for the whole expression to be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 4 D

Correct answer: D. ¬p ∨ q

Explanation: The conditional p → q is logically equivalent to ¬p ∨ q.

Quick lesson: Remember: “if p then q” can be rewritten as “not p OR q.”

Symbolic Logic — Question 5 C

Correct answer: C. If a student does not pass, the student does not study.

Explanation: The expression ¬q → ¬p reads: if q is false, then p is false.

Quick lesson: Read the symbols in order: ¬ means not, and → means if...then.

Symbolic Logic — Question 6 A

Correct answer: A. q → p

Explanation: The converse is formed by switching the hypothesis and conclusion of p → q.

Quick lesson: To form the converse, switch the two sides of the arrow.

Symbolic Logic — Question 7 D

Correct answer: D. ¬q → ¬p

Explanation: The contrapositive switches the two parts and negates both: ¬q → ¬p.

Quick lesson: To form the contrapositive, switch the sides and add NOT to each.

Symbolic Logic — Question 8 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: An OR statement is true if at least one part is true. Here q is true, so p ∨ q is true.

Quick lesson: For inclusive OR, only one true statement is needed; both may also be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 9 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: “Neither p nor q” means p is false AND q is false.

Quick lesson: Translate “neither...nor” as NOT the first AND NOT the second.

Symbolic Logic — Question 10 B

Correct answer: B. False

Explanation: A conditional is false only when its first part is true and its second part is false. That is exactly the given situation.

Quick lesson: For p → q, the only false case is TRUE → FALSE.

Symbolic Logic — Question 11 D

Correct answer: D. (p ∨ q) ∧ ¬(p ∧ q)

Explanation: The expression requires at least one of p or q to be true, while also excluding the case where both are true.

Quick lesson: “Either...or, but not both” is exclusive OR: one is true and the other is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 12 B

Correct answer: B. ¬p → ¬q

Explanation: The inverse is formed by negating both parts of the original conditional without switching their order.

Quick lesson: For the inverse, keep the order and place NOT before both statements.

Symbolic Logic — Question 13 A

Correct answer: A. True

Explanation: A biconditional is true when both statements have the same truth value. Both p and q are true.

Quick lesson: For p ↔ q, matching truth values (both true or both false) make it true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 14 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: By De Morgan’s law, ¬(p ∨ q) is equivalent to ¬p ∧ ¬q.

Quick lesson: To negate an OR statement, change OR to AND and negate both parts.

Symbolic Logic — Question 15 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: First, p ∧ q is false because q is false. Then FALSE ∨ TRUE is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate parentheses first, then apply the outside operator.

Symbolic Logic — Question 16 D

Correct answer: D. p → q

Explanation: “Accepted only if qualified” means being qualified is necessary for acceptance: if accepted, then qualified.

Quick lesson: “P only if Q” translates to P → Q. The phrase after “only if” is the necessary condition.

Symbolic Logic — Question 17 A

Correct answer: A. True

Explanation: A biconditional is true when both sides have the same truth value. Both p and q are false.

Quick lesson: For ↔, both false is also a matching pair, so the result is true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 18 B

Correct answer: B. If p, then both q and r.

Explanation: The expression p → (q ∧ r) means that whenever p occurs, q and r must both be true.

Quick lesson: The symbol ∧ means AND. When it is inside the consequent, both conditions must hold.

Symbolic Logic — Question 19 C

Correct answer: C. p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: A conditional p → q is false only when p is true and q is false. That condition is p ∧ ¬q.

Quick lesson: The negation of “if p then q” is “p is true AND q is false.”

Symbolic Logic — Question 20 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: Since p is true, p ∨ r is true. Since q is also true, the conjunction is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the OR inside the parentheses first, then combine its result with q using AND.

Symbolic Logic — Question 21 A

Correct answer: A. p → (q ∧ r)

Explanation: The machine operating implies that both electricity and fuel are available.

Quick lesson: Translate “if p, then q and r” as p → (q ∧ r).

Symbolic Logic — Question 22 B

Correct answer: B. p ∨ ¬p

Explanation: A tautology is true for every possible truth value. Either p is true or p is not true, so p ∨ ¬p is always true.

Quick lesson: A statement joined with its own negation by OR is always true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 23 C

Correct answer: C. False

Explanation: Since p is true, ¬p is false. Since q is false, FALSE ∨ FALSE is false.

Quick lesson: Negate p first, then evaluate the OR. OR is false only when both parts are false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 24 D

Correct answer: D. ¬(p ∧ q)

Explanation: The statement says it is not true that both applicants are qualified, so negate the conjunction of p and q.

Quick lesson: “Not both” means NOT (p AND q), not necessarily that both are unqualified.

Symbolic Logic — Question 25 D

Correct answer: D. True

Explanation: A true conditional with a true hypothesis must have a true conclusion. Therefore q must be true.

Quick lesson: For a conditional to remain true when p is true, q must also be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 26 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: Since p is false, ¬p is true. q is also true, so their conjunction is true.

Quick lesson: Negate p first. TRUE AND TRUE gives TRUE.

Symbolic Logic — Question 27 C

Correct answer: C. p ↔ q

Explanation: “If and only if” is represented by the biconditional symbol ↔.

Quick lesson: Translate “if and only if” directly as ↔. It expresses both directions.

Symbolic Logic — Question 28 A

Correct answer: A. ¬p ∨ ¬q

Explanation: By De Morgan’s law, the negation of p ∧ q is ¬p ∨ ¬q.

Quick lesson: Negating an AND changes it to OR and negates each statement.

Symbolic Logic — Question 29 D

Correct answer: D. False

Explanation: If q is false and p were true, p → q would be false. Since the conditional is true, p must be false.

Quick lesson: Use the only false case of a conditional: TRUE → FALSE. To avoid it when q is false, p must be false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 30 B

Correct answer: B. If the guard responds, then the alarm rings.

Explanation: The converse switches the original condition and result. Original: alarm → response. Converse: response → alarm.

Quick lesson: To form the converse, reverse the order of the two parts without negating them.

Symbolic Logic — Question 31 C

Correct answer: C. False

Explanation: q ∨ r is false because both q and r are false. Then p AND false is false.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the OR inside parentheses first. A true value AND false gives false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 32 A

Correct answer: A. p → q

Explanation: “Not hired unless passes” means that passing is necessary for being hired: if the applicant is hired, the applicant passed.

Quick lesson: “P unless Q” can be read as “if not Q, then not P,” which is equivalent to P → Q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 33 D

Correct answer: D. If a number is not even, then it is not divisible by 4.

Explanation: The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p. Here, q means the number is even, so not q means it is not even.

Quick lesson: Form the contrapositive by switching the parts and negating both.

Symbolic Logic — Question 34 B

Correct answer: B. False

Explanation: A biconditional is false when its two sides have different truth values. True and false do not match.

Quick lesson: For ↔, matching values make true; different values make false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 35 C

Correct answer: C. p ∨ q

Explanation: “At least one” means p is true, q is true, or both are true. This is inclusive OR.

Quick lesson: The inclusive OR symbol ∨ allows either one or both statements to be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 36 B

Correct answer: B. False

Explanation: p → q is true because both p and q are true. But ¬q is false, so TRUE ∧ FALSE is false.

Quick lesson: Evaluate each part first. A conjunction is false if either part is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 37 D

Correct answer: D. ¬q → ¬p

Explanation: The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p, and a conditional is logically equivalent to its contrapositive.

Quick lesson: A conditional and its contrapositive always have the same truth value.

Symbolic Logic — Question 38 B

Correct answer: B. An applicant passes the examination.

Explanation: In p → q, p is the condition stated first: the applicant passes the examination. q is the result: the applicant is qualified.

Quick lesson: When a statement is written as “if p, then q,” identify p as the if-part and q as the then-part.

Symbolic Logic — Question 39 C

Correct answer: C. If the student does not attend class, then the student does not learn the lesson.

Explanation: The inverse negates both parts without switching them: ¬p → ¬q.

Quick lesson: The inverse keeps the original order but places NOT before both statements.

Symbolic Logic — Question 40 C

Correct answer: C. True

Explanation: Since p and q are both false, p ∨ q is false. A conditional with a false hypothesis is true, so false → true is true.

Quick lesson: A conditional is automatically true when its hypothesis is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 41 B

Correct answer: B. p → q

Explanation: “The system works only if electricity is available” means working requires electricity. If the system works, electricity must be available.

Quick lesson: “P only if Q” means P → Q, because Q is necessary for P.

Symbolic Logic — Question 42 C

Correct answer: C. At least one employee is not punctual.

Explanation: The negation of “every employee is punctual” is that there is at least one employee who is not punctual.

Quick lesson: To negate “all,” use “at least one is not.” It does not mean nobody is punctual.

Symbolic Logic — Question 43 B

Correct answer: B. p ∧ ¬p

Explanation: A contradiction is always false. A statement and its negation cannot both be true, so p ∧ ¬p is a contradiction.

Quick lesson: A statement AND its own NOT is always false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 44 B

Correct answer: B. p → r

Explanation: If p implies q, and q implies r, then p also implies r. This is the transitive property of implication.

Quick lesson: Chain conditional statements: p → q and q → r give p → r.

Symbolic Logic — Question 45 C

Correct answer: C. p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: The applicant is qualified (p) AND did not submit the documents (¬q). The word “but” functions like AND in logic.

Quick lesson: Translate “but” as AND, then negate the statement that follows “did not.”

Symbolic Logic — Question 46 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: p ↔ r is true because both p and r are true. Also, ¬q is true because q is false. TRUE ∧ TRUE is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the biconditional and negation first, then combine them with AND.

Symbolic Logic — Question 47 D

Correct answer: D. p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: The negation of p → q is p ∧ ¬q. This is the one situation that makes a conditional false.

Quick lesson: A conditional fails only when the first part is true and the second part is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 48 A

Correct answer: A. The original conditional statement is false.

Explanation: The student studied (the condition is true) but did not pass (the result is false). That is the one case in which “if I study, I will pass” is false.

Quick lesson: A conditional is disproved by a true hypothesis followed by a false conclusion.

Symbolic Logic — Question 49 B

Correct answer: B. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: “Neither the manager nor the supervisor” means the manager did not approve AND the supervisor did not approve.

Quick lesson: Translate “neither...nor” as NOT p AND NOT q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 50 C

Correct answer: C. False

Explanation: A biconditional is true when both sides have the same truth value. Since p is false and the biconditional is true, q must also be false.

Quick lesson: If p ↔ q is true, p and q must match. If one is false, the other is false too.

Symbolic Logic — Question 51 A

Correct answer: A. True

Explanation: p → q is false because p is true and q is false. But r ∧ p is true because both r and p are true. Therefore FALSE ∨ TRUE is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate each part first, then apply OR. OR is true when at least one side is true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 52 B

Correct answer: B. p → (q ∧ r)

Explanation: “Can enter only if” means entry requires both an identification card and authorization. Therefore, if the person enters, both q and r must be true.

Quick lesson: “P only if Q” means P → Q. If there are two requirements joined by AND, write P → (Q ∧ R).

Symbolic Logic — Question 53 C

Correct answer: C. p → (q ∧ r)

Explanation: Both conditionals share the same antecedent p. Whenever p is true, both q and r must be true, which is p → (q ∧ r).

Quick lesson: Combine conditionals with the same starting condition by joining their conclusions with AND.

Symbolic Logic — Question 54 A

Correct answer: A. p and r have the same truth value.

Explanation: If p ↔ q is true, p and q match. If q ↔ r is also true, q and r match. Therefore p and r must match.

Quick lesson: A true biconditional means its two statements have identical truth values.

Symbolic Logic — Question 55 D

Correct answer: D. Every employee fails to be both hardworking and punctual.

Explanation: The negation of “some employees are both hardworking and punctual” is that no employee satisfies both qualities together. This is expressed by option D.

Quick lesson: To negate “some,” use “every” or “no.” Negate the whole combined condition, not necessarily each quality separately.

Symbolic Logic — Question 56 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: p ∨ r is false because both p and r are false. Its negation is true. Since q is true, TRUE ↔ TRUE is true.

Quick lesson: A biconditional is true when both sides have the same truth value.

Symbolic Logic — Question 57 A

Correct answer: A. (p ∧ q) → r

Explanation: The expression says that if p is true, then q must imply r. This is equivalent to saying that if both p and q are true, r must be true.

Quick lesson: A nested conditional p → (q → r) can be rewritten as (p ∧ q) → r.

Symbolic Logic — Question 58 A

Correct answer: A. p → (q ∧ r)

Explanation: “May graduate if” the student completes all subjects and passes means those two requirements together are sufficient for graduation: (q ∧ r) → p. Wait: the exact phrase places the requirements after “if,” so the correct expression is (q ∧ r) → p.

Quick lesson: In “P if Q,” Q is the condition that leads to P. This is Q → P; do not confuse it with “P only if Q.”

Symbolic Logic — Question 59 C

Correct answer: C. If p → q and q is false, then p is false.

Explanation: This is modus tollens: if p implies q, and q is false, p cannot be true.

Quick lesson: Modus tollens follows this pattern: p → q; ¬q; therefore ¬p.

Symbolic Logic — Question 60 B

Correct answer: B. Always true.

Explanation: If p ∧ q is true, then both p and q are true, so p ∨ q is also true. If p ∧ q is false, the conditional has a false antecedent and is true.

Quick lesson: A conditional with a false antecedent is true. This expression never produces false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 61 C

Correct answer: C. ¬(p ∨ q)

Explanation: The statement denies the entire event “p or q.” Therefore, negate the whole disjunction: ¬(p ∨ q).

Quick lesson: When the phrase is “it is false that [whole statement],” put NOT around the entire expression.

Symbolic Logic — Question 62 B

Correct answer: B. p is TRUE and q is FALSE.

Explanation: A conditional p → q is false in exactly one case: p true and q false.

Quick lesson: Memorize the conditional truth rule: only TRUE → FALSE makes a conditional false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 63 D

Correct answer: D. p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: The negation of p → q is the situation where p is true but q is false.

Quick lesson: The negation of an implication is p AND NOT q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 64 B

Correct answer: B. If the server is overloaded, users experience delays.

Explanation: The first statement gives overloaded server → slow website. The second gives slow website → user delays. Chaining them gives overloaded server → user delays.

Quick lesson: Use hypothetical syllogism: if p → q and q → r, then p → r.

Symbolic Logic — Question 65 A

Correct answer: A. (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∧ ¬q)

Explanation: The expression requires at least one of p or q to be true while also requiring both to be false. These conditions cannot happen together, so it is a contradiction.

Quick lesson: A contradiction is false in every possible case. Look for conditions that cannot be true simultaneously.

Symbolic Logic — Question 66 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: p ↔ q is true because both are true. Since r is false, ¬r is true. A true antecedent leading to a true consequent makes the conditional true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the biconditional and NOT first, then evaluate the conditional.

Symbolic Logic — Question 67 D

Correct answer: D. p ↔ q

Explanation: “Exactly when” means “if and only if,” which is represented by the biconditional symbol ↔.

Quick lesson: The phrases “if and only if,” “exactly when,” and “necessary and sufficient” indicate ↔.

Symbolic Logic — Question 68 A

Correct answer: A. If a number is not divisible by 6, then it is not divisible by 3.

Explanation: The inverse negates both parts of the original statement without switching their order.

Quick lesson: For the inverse of p → q, write ¬p → ¬q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 69 A

Correct answer: A. p ∨ (q ∧ r)

Explanation: By the distributive law, (p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ r) is equivalent to p ∨ (q ∧ r).

Quick lesson: A useful distributive identity is (p OR q) AND (p OR r) = p OR (q AND r).

Symbolic Logic — Question 70 C

Correct answer: C. p ↔ (q ∨ r)

Explanation: “If and only if” means a biconditional, and “meets the sales target or receives an outstanding rating” is q ∨ r.

Quick lesson: Translate “if and only if” as ↔ and inclusive “or” as ∨.

Symbolic Logic — Question 71 D

Correct answer: D. p → r

Explanation: The two true conditionals can be chained: p → q and q → r imply p → r.

Quick lesson: When the conclusion of one conditional is the starting condition of another, chain them.

Symbolic Logic — Question 72 B and C

Correct answer: B and C are both correct.

Explanation: The negation of a biconditional is true when p and q have different truth values. Option B states this as the two exclusive cases. Option C is another equivalent expression for the same exclusive-OR condition.

Quick lesson: A biconditional is false when its sides differ. Its negation is XOR. **Question issue:** both B and C express XOR, so the item has two correct choices and should be revised.

Symbolic Logic — Question 73 A

Correct answer: A. Maria is qualified.

Explanation: All licensed teachers are qualified, and Maria is a licensed teacher. Therefore, Maria belongs to the group of qualified people.

Quick lesson: From “All A are B” and “x is A,” conclude “x is B.”

Symbolic Logic — Question 74 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: “Neither the machine is defective nor the operator is inexperienced” means both statements are false.

Quick lesson: “Neither p nor q” translates to ¬p AND ¬q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 75 B

Correct answer: B. p is FALSE and q is FALSE.

Explanation: Since ¬r is true, r is false. From q → r, q must be false; otherwise a true q with false r would make that conditional false. Then p → q and q false require p to be false too.

Quick lesson: Apply modus tollens twice: q → r with ¬r gives ¬q; then p → q with ¬q gives ¬p.

Symbolic Logic — Question 76 B

Correct answer: B. p is true and q is false.

Explanation: An implication is false only when its antecedent is true and its consequent is false.

Quick lesson: The only false row for p → q is TRUE → FALSE.

Symbolic Logic — Question 77 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∨ ¬q

Explanation: By De Morgan’s law, NOT (p AND q) becomes NOT p OR NOT q.

Quick lesson: To negate an AND, switch it to OR and negate both statements.

Symbolic Logic — Question 78 B

Correct answer: B. Maria did not pass the examination.

Explanation: If passing implies qualifying, then not qualifying means the applicant did not pass. This is modus tollens.

Quick lesson: From p → q and ¬q, conclude ¬p.

Symbolic Logic — Question 79 D

Correct answer: D. q → p

Explanation: The converse of p → q switches the antecedent and consequent.

Quick lesson: To form the converse, reverse the two sides of the arrow.

Symbolic Logic — Question 80 A

Correct answer: A. True

Explanation: p ∨ q is true because p is true. ¬q is also true because q is false. TRUE ∧ TRUE is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the OR and NOT first, then combine the results with AND.

Symbolic Logic — Question 81 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: “Neither p nor q is true” means p is false and q is false.

Quick lesson: Translate “neither...nor” as NOT p AND NOT q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 82 B

Correct answer: B. p → r

Explanation: From p → q and q → r, the implication p → r follows by chaining the two statements.

Quick lesson: This is hypothetical syllogism: p → q, q → r, therefore p → r.

Symbolic Logic — Question 83 A

Correct answer: A. (p ∧ q) ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)

Explanation: A biconditional is true when both statements are true or when both are false. Option A lists exactly those two cases.

Quick lesson: Remember: p ↔ q means both have matching truth values.

Symbolic Logic — Question 84 D

Correct answer: D. True

Explanation: A conditional with a false antecedent is true, regardless of whether its consequent is true or false.

Quick lesson: When p is false, p → q is true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 85 B

Correct answer: B. The machine is not defective.

Explanation: If the machine is defective, production stops. Since production has not stopped, the machine cannot be defective. This is modus tollens.

Quick lesson: From p → q and ¬q, infer ¬p. Do not infer the converse.

Symbolic Logic — Question 86 C

Correct answer: C. ¬p → ¬q

Explanation: The inverse negates both the antecedent and consequent while keeping their order.

Quick lesson: Inverse = negate both parts without switching them.

Symbolic Logic — Question 87 B

Correct answer: B. False

Explanation: p ∧ q is true because both p and q are true. The conditional TRUE → FALSE is false because r is false.

Quick lesson: A conditional is false only when its first part is true and its second part is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 88 A

Correct answer: A. ¬p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: De Morgan’s law changes NOT (p OR q) into NOT p AND NOT q.

Quick lesson: To negate an OR, change OR to AND and negate each part.

Symbolic Logic — Question 89 C

Correct answer: C. q

Explanation: From p → q and p, q must be true. This is the rule called modus ponens.

Quick lesson: Modus ponens: p → q; p; therefore q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 90 B

Correct answer: B. p ∨ ¬p

Explanation: Either p is true or p is not true. One of these must always be true, regardless of p’s value.

Quick lesson: A statement OR its negation is a tautology.

Symbolic Logic — Question 91 D

Correct answer: D. True

Explanation: Because p is true and p → q is true, q must also be true.

Quick lesson: A true antecedent in a true conditional forces the consequent to be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 92 C

Correct answer: C. p → ¬q

Explanation: The phrase “if p, then not q” places p before the arrow and negates q.

Quick lesson: Translate “if p, then...” as p →; then add NOT to q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 93 D

Correct answer: D. All registered voters are citizens.

Explanation: This is exactly the first given premise. The second premise only says some citizens are teachers; it does not establish that any registered voters are teachers.

Quick lesson: Do not assume that two groups overlap just because they share a broader category. Only the original universal statement is guaranteed.

Symbolic Logic — Question 94 A

Correct answer: A. ¬p ∨ q

Explanation: The conditional p → q is equivalent to ¬p ∨ q.

Quick lesson: Memorize this identity: IF p THEN q = NOT p OR q.

Symbolic Logic — Question 95 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: A biconditional is true when both statements have the same truth value. Both p and q are false, so they match.

Quick lesson: For ↔, both true or both false gives true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 96 C

Correct answer: C. If p, then q; not q; therefore not p.

Explanation: Modus tollens starts with p → q, denies the consequent, and concludes that the antecedent is false.

Quick lesson: Modus tollens: p → q; ¬q; therefore ¬p.

Symbolic Logic — Question 97 D

Correct answer: D. q

Explanation: Since p OR q is true and p is false, q must be true for the disjunction to remain true.

Quick lesson: In an OR statement, if one side is false and the whole expression is true, the other side must be true.

Symbolic Logic — Question 98 A

Correct answer: A. p ∧ ¬q

Explanation: The negation of p → q is p ∧ ¬q.

Quick lesson: An implication is false exactly when p is true and q is false.

Symbolic Logic — Question 99 B

Correct answer: B. True

Explanation: p ∧ r is true because both p and r are true. TRUE OR FALSE is true.

Quick lesson: Evaluate the AND inside the parentheses first, then apply OR.

Symbolic Logic — Question 100 C

Correct answer: C. The employee receives feedback.

Explanation: The employee submits the report, so the supervisor reviews it. Since the supervisor reviews it, the employee receives feedback. Both implications can be chained.

Quick lesson: Use a chain of conditionals: p → q, q → r, and p give r.
